=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array of strings words and a string target.
A string x is called valid if x is a prefix of any string in words.
Return the minimum number of valid strings that can be concatenated to form target. If it is not possible to form target, return -1.
 
Example 1:

Input: words = ["abc","aaaaa","bcdef"], target = "aabcdabc"
Output: 3
Explanation:
The target string can be formed by concatenating:

Prefix of length 2 of words[1], i.e. "aa".
Prefix of length 3 of words[2], i.e. "bcd".
Prefix of length 3 of words[0], i.e. "abc".


Example 2:

Input: words = ["abababab","ab"], target = "ababaababa"
Output: 2
Explanation:
The target string can be formed by concatenating:

Prefix of length 5 of words[0], i.e. "ababa".
Prefix of length 5 of words[0], i.e. "ababa".


Example 3:

Input: words = ["abcdef"], target = "xyz"
Output: -1

 
Constraints:

1 <= words.length <= 100
1 <= words[i].length <= 5 * 10^3
The input is generated such that sum(words[i].length) <= 10^5.
words[i] consists only of lowercase English letters.
1 <= target.length <= 5 * 10^3
target consists only of lowercase English letters.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minValidStrings(self, words: List[str], target: str) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's define:
class Solution:
    def minValidStrings(self, words: List[str], target: str) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for w in words:
            node = trie
            for ch in w:
                if ch not in node:
                    node[ch] = {}
                node = node[ch]
        n = len(target)
        INF = 10**9
        dp = [INF]*(n+1)
        dp[0]=0
        # For each start i with dp[i] finite, compute reach and update dp.
        for i in range(n):
            if dp[i] == INF:
                continue
            node = trie
            # max reach length L
            # We can update as we traverse: for length l from 1.. while target[i+l-1] in node
            # dp[i+l] = min(dp[i+l], dp[i]+1)
            # But if dp[i] finite, all shorter positions maybe finite? dp[i] finite. Need update all j.
            # Could compute reach first then loop. Or update on fly.
            l = 0
            j = i
            while j < n and target[j] in node:
                node = node[target[j]]
                j += 1
                l += 1
                if dp[i] + 1 < dp[j]:
                    dp[j] = dp[i] + 1
            # Actually if dp[i] finite, update every prefix length l. Good.
        return -1 if dp[n] == INF else dp[n]

Now, final code can be:

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minValidStrings(self, words: List[str], target: str) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for w in words:
            node = trie
            for ch in w:
                if ch not in node:
                    node[ch] = {}
                node = node[ch]
        
        n = len(target)
        INF = 10**9
        dp = [INF] * (n + 1)
        dp[0] = 0
        
        for i in range(n):
            if dp[i] == INF:
                continue
            node = trie
            j = i
            while j < n:
                ch = target[j]
                if ch not in node:
                    break
                node = node[ch]
                j += 1
                if dp[i] + 1 < dp[j]:
                    dp[j] = dp[i] + 1
        
        return -1 if dp[n] == INF else dp[n]

A word prefix corresponds to a suffix of the word, not a suffix of the concatenated string? Actually if we concatenate words, a prefix of a word is a substring starting at the word's start, not at the end. In a suffix array of the concatenated string, suffixes starting at word starts have prefixes equal to word prefixes. We need for each target suffix, max LCP with any suffix of the concatenated string that starts at a word start. This can be answered by suffix array: For each target suffix, find its position in suffix array and nearest word-start suffix, take LCP. But building suffix array in Python for length 1e5+5e3 maybe possible but complex. Rolling hash binary search easier.

Rolling hash approach:
- Precompute all prefixes of all words as strings? There are up to 1e5 prefixes. We can store their hash values in a set. But to compute hash of target substring quickly, need rolling hash.
- For each start i in target, binary search max L such that hash(target[i:i+L]) in set. Need also ensure L <= max word length and L <= n-i. Since set contains hashes of all prefixes up to word length. If hash collision not considered, okay. To avoid collision, use double 64-bit or Python tuple of two mod hashes. But in competitive programming, randomized 64-bit accepted. However, deterministic? We can use Python's built-in `hash`? Not stable across runs but within run stable; could use `hash(s)` for substring? Slicing creates string O(L), not okay. Could use polynomial hash with two mod primes. But collisions theoretically possible; usually accepted but not mathematically rigorous. The problem likely expects trie DP, not hash.

Could use a trie and "binary lifting" to jump? Another deterministic way: Build trie. For each i, we can traverse from root until mismatch. To avoid O(total matched length), we can precompute for each trie node and each target position? Not.

We can process target with a "trie of words" and use failure links? Let's think of Aho-Corasick in reverse direction.

We have a trie T of words. We want to know for each position i in target, the deepest node reached by following target[i], target[i+1], ... from root. This is equivalent to finding all substrings of target that are prefixes of words, but only those starting at i. If we build AC over the set of all words (patterns), and scan target, at each position j we find all patterns that end at j. A pattern is a word; if a word ends at j, then its prefix? Not helpful. But if we insert all prefixes of words as patterns into AC, then scanning target would find all occurrences of any prefix ending at j. That tells us for each ending position j, the set of starting positions i such that target[i:j] is a prefix of a word. We need for each i the maximum j. We could process all occurrences, but there can be O(n^2) occurrences (e.g., target all 'a', all prefixes "a","aa",...). AC would report many. But we only need max j for each i. There is a known technique: Build AC over all words, scan target, maintain for each position j the longest pattern ending at j. But again gives ending positions, not starting. However, if a pattern is a prefix of a word, and it ends at j, then its start is j - len(pattern) + 1. We need for each start i the max len. We could update ans[start] with len for each occurrence, but too many.

Could use AC to find all occurrences of words in target, but valid strings are prefixes of words, not necessarily full words. If a word occurs ending at j, then its prefixes occur ending at earlier positions, not same start. Not enough.

Another deterministic approach: Use suffix tree of target. We need for each word prefix, find its occurrences in target; then for each start i, max length. We can build suffix automaton of target and traverse each word prefix to find all positions? Too many.

Given constraints, simple trie DP is intended.

But let's further explore the advanced algorithm in case the target length is actually 5e4 and the simple solution might fail. Could we implement a more efficient solution that is still not too complex and deterministic? Let's see.

We need compute R_i for all i. We can use rolling hash with binary search. Then DP with range min updates. Range min updates can be done with a segment tree or difference? We need update dp[l..r] with value v (dp[i]+1), and query dp[j] at each j. Since updates are range min and point queries, we can use a lazy segment tree or a priority queue? Actually dp[j] = min over intervals covering j of value. We process i increasing, and for each i with dp[i] known, add interval [i+1, R_i] with value dp[i]+1. Then dp[j] is min value among intervals covering j. We can compute dp sequentially with a min-heap of active intervals: At position j, before computing dp[j], add all intervals starting at j? Wait intervals start at i+1. We process j from 1 to n. For each start i, interval [i+1, R_i] becomes active at j=i+1 and expires after R_i. We can store events at start i+1: add (value, end=R_i). At position j, add intervals with start == j, remove expired (end < j), dp[j] = min value in heap. This is O(n log n) and avoids range update segment tree. But to compute R_i, we need hash. This would handle n=5e4.

But if we use trie traversal to compute R_i, total O(n^2) maybe too high. Rolling hash binary search O(n log n) with set of prefix hashes. Let's examine correctness and implementation details.

Precompute all prefixes of all words:
prefix_hashes = set()
For each word w:
    h = 0
    for ch in w:
        h = (h * base + val(ch)) % mod
        prefix_hashes.add(h)
But if two different prefixes have same hash due to collision, false positives. Use double mod or 64-bit. To be safer, use Python tuple of two mods. But storing up to 1e5 tuples okay. For target substring hash:
pref[i+1] = hash of target[:i+1] under same base/mod.
hash(s[l:r]) = pref[r] - pref[l]*base^(r-l) mod mod.
Then binary search for each i:
lo=0, hi=min(max_word_len, n-i)
while lo<hi:
    mid=(lo+hi+1)//2
    if hash(target[i:i+mid]) in prefix_hashes:
        lo=mid
    else:
        hi=mid-1
R_i = lo.
Need ensure prefix_hashes contains all prefixes of length up to max_word_len. Yes.

But there is a subtlety: If a prefix hash appears due to a prefix of a word, then target substring of that hash is valid. If collision, false positive. Double mod reduces. Could also store actual strings? Not feasible. Could use Python's built-in `hash` of a substring? Slicing O(L). Could use `memoryview`? no.

Could we avoid collisions deterministically by using a trie and binary search with "jump" using hashing? No.

Another deterministic method: Use suffix array to compute R_i. Let's explore if implementable.

We have a set of strings: all prefixes of words. We want for each target suffix, longest common prefix with any prefix of a word. This is equivalent to: Build a generalized suffix automaton or suffix tree of the set of words and target? Actually a prefix of a word is a substring starting at 0. If we create a string S = word1 + '{' + word2 + '{' + ... + '{' + target, then a prefix of a word is a substring starting at the beginning of a word. For each target suffix (starting at position pos in target), we need max LCP with any suffix of S that starts at a word start. We can build a suffix array for S and LCP array. Then for each target suffix, find its rank in suffix array. The maximum LCP with any word-start suffix is the maximum of LCP with nearest word-start suffix to the left and right in the suffix array (because LCP with any suffix in a range is min of LCPs; maximum occurs adjacent to nearest word-start suffix? Actually for a fixed suffix, the LCP with any other suffix is the minimum LCP over the interval between their ranks. The maximum LCP with any suffix from a set W is achieved by one of the nearest W suffixes in rank order? I think yes: The LCP between suffix x and any suffix y is min of LCP array between ranks. If there is a W suffix to the left and right, the maximum over all W is max of LCP with nearest W on each side? Let's verify. For suffix array, LCP(x, y) = min_{k=rank(x)+1..rank(y)} LCP[k] if rank(x)<rank(y). As y moves away, the min over interval is non-increasing? Actually if you move away, the interval expands, min can only decrease or stay same. So the maximum LCP with any W suffix is attained at the closest W suffix on either side (in rank order). Yes. So we can precompute nearest word-start suffix rank to left/right and LCP queries (RMQ) to get R_i. This is deterministic but implementing suffix array for length ~1e5+5e3 in Python is possible but lengthy. Not needed.

Another deterministic method: Build a trie and use "binary lifting on trie nodes" with target? Not.

Before final, let's maybe test the code mentally on a tricky case where greedy fails:
words = ["aaaaaaaaaa", "aaaaa"]? Need construct target length 100. But target length max 5000. Suppose target = "a"*100, words = ["a"*10, "a"*50]? Choosing shorter at 0 (1) then at 1 R1=100, total 2. Same. Need a case where choosing max at 0 leads to a bad position, but choosing shorter leads to a much better position, and max position is bad. Is that possible with prefix property? Let's analyze.

We have intervals from each position i to R_i. We want min segments to reach n. Greedy max reach at current position may fail for general interval covering where intervals can start at any position? But here intervals start only at current position, and you can choose any end <= R_i. This is equivalent to choosing next position j in (i, R_i]. Greedy choosing j=R_i. Could choosing a smaller j allow a later interval with much larger reach, while j=R_i has small reach? Yes, in general interval covering with intervals starting at every position, greedy max reach is optimal for covering a line if intervals can start at any position <= current? Wait classic interval covering problem: Given intervals [l,r] on a line, select min intervals to cover [0,n]. Greedy: at current covered position, choose interval with l <= current and maximum r. That is optimal. But here intervals are only [i, R_i]? Actually a segment from i to j corresponds to interval [i, j] (covers positions i to j). To cover [0,n] with intervals, intervals can start at any boundary. The classic greedy for interval covering chooses among intervals whose start <= current position, not necessarily equal. Here at current position i, we can choose any interval starting at i? But we could also choose an interval that started earlier and extends beyond i, which would have covered i without a boundary at i. In DP, dp[i] finite may be due to an interval that started earlier and spans over i, but if we are at position i as a boundary, we choose a new interval starting at i. Greedy max reach at each boundary might fail? Let's try to construct.

General interval covering with intervals [l,r] where l can be < current. Greedy max r among intervals with l <= current is optimal. In our problem, if we are at boundary i, intervals with l <= i and r > i could be used to cover from i onward without a new segment? But concatenation requires segments to start at previous boundary. If an interval started earlier and spans over i, then i is not a boundary; we wouldn't be "at i" as a boundary. If we choose to make i a boundary, then the previous segment ended at i, and the next segment must start at i. So intervals starting earlier are not available. Thus the classic greedy doesn't directly apply.

Can we construct a counterexample to greedy max reach at each boundary? Let's try with target strings and prefixes.

We need positions 0..n. At pos 0, R0 = a > 1. If greedy goes to a, then R_a is small. If choose b < a, then R_b is large enough to reach n, and total segments 2. Greedy would need maybe 3 or fail. Is this possible with prefix property?

Let's define target string T. R_i is longest L such that T[i:i+L] is a prefix of some word. We can choose any words to satisfy R values. We need T[0:a] prefix of some word W0 (so R0 >= a). We need T[a:n] not coverable well (R_a small). We need T[b:n] prefix of some word Wb (so R_b >= n-b). Also T[0:b] must be prefix of some word? Since R0 >= a > b, T[0:b] is prefix of W0, so valid. Thus we can choose segment length b from 0, then segment T[b:n] from b. Total 2. Greedy chooses a, then at a, R_a small, maybe cannot reach n in one more, so total >2 or fail. Is this allowed? Yes, choose W0 with prefix T[0:a], Wb with prefix T[b:n], and ensure no word has prefix T[a:n] (or R_a small). But note W0 has prefix T[0:a], and since b < a, W0 also has prefix T[0:b]. That's fine. Does W0 impose any constraints on R_b? No. Does Wb impose constraints on R0? Wb has prefix T[b:n]. Does that imply some prefix of T[0:n] is valid? Not necessarily, because T[b:n] is not related to T[0:n] except it starts at b. So R0 could be exactly a if no word has prefix T[0:a+1]. We can choose W0 = T[0:a] (full word), so R0 = a (unless other words extend). Choose Wb = T[b:n] (full word), so R_b = n-b. Choose no other words. Then R_a: need T[a:n] prefix of any word? W0 = T[0:a] is too short; Wb = T[b:n] starts with T[b], not T[a] generally. So R_a could be 0 if T[a] not equal T[b]? Wait R_a is longest prefix of T[a:] that is prefix of any word. Words are W0=T[0:a] and Wb=T[b:n]. The prefixes of W0 are T[0:k] for k<=a. For T[a:] to match a prefix of W0, we need T[a] == T[0], T[a+1]==T[1], etc. Could happen for some length but likely not long. Prefixes of Wb are T[b:b+k]. Need T[a] == T[b], etc. We can choose T to avoid. Thus greedy can fail.

Let's instantiate with concrete strings. Let n=5, a=2, b=1. T = "ababa"? Need T[0:2]="ab" W0="ab". T[1:5]="baba" Wb="baba". R0=2 (word "ab" only; but Wb prefix "b" not "a", so R0=2). Greedy at 0 chooses length 2 -> pos2. At pos2, T[2:5]="aba". Is "aba" prefix of any word? W0="ab" too short; Wb="baba" prefixes: "b","ba","bab","baba"; none "a". So R2=0. Greedy fails to reach 5. But optimal: choose length 1 from 0 ("a" prefix of W0), then from 1 choose length 4 ("baba" prefix of Wb), total 2. Is this valid? Let's check words = ["ab", "baba"], target = "ababa". Valid strings: prefixes of "ab": "a","ab"; prefixes of "baba": "b","ba","bab","baba". Target "ababa" can be formed by "a" + "baba" = "ababa" (2 strings). Greedy max reach at 0: R0=2 because "ab" valid, but after "ab", remaining "aba" cannot start with any valid string (no word starts with 'a' except "ab", but "aba" too long; "a" valid but then "ba" valid? Wait at pos2, T[2:5]="aba". If we choose "a" at pos2, then pos3, T[3:5]="ba" valid, total 3. So greedy max reach 2 gives 3 segments, not optimal 2. Thus greedy fails. Good. Our DP would find 2. Let's test DP:
words ["ab","baba"], target "ababa".
Trie root a,b.
i=0: target "ababa": a->b, then target[2]='a' after "ab" no child? word "ab" ends, no child; R0=2. dp1=1, dp2=1.
i=1: dp1=1, target[1:]="baba": root b -> a -> b -> a (word baba) R1=4. update dp2=min(1,2)=1, dp3=2, dp4=2, dp5=2. Return 2. Good. So DP needed. Greedy not.

This also shows that dp[i] finite for all i? dp3=2, dp4=2, dp5=2. Yes.

Now, let's think about if our DP would process i=2 with dp2=1, target[2:]="aba": root a -> b, then target[4]='a' after "ab" no child; R2=2? Actually target[2:4]="ab" valid, target[4]='a' after "ab" no child, so R2=2. update dp3=min(2,2)=2, dp4=min(2,2)=2. i=3 dp3=2, target[3:]="ba": root b -> a, R3=2, update dp4=min(2,3)=2, dp5=min(2,3)=2. Return 2. Good.

This counterexample confirms DP.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "minimum number" if target can be formed by concatenating valid strings but the DP returns a value greater than actual due to not considering segments that start at i but dp[i] is INF? We argued impossible. Let's prove more formally:
If there exists a partition of target[0:n] into valid strings with boundaries 0 = p0 < p1 < ... < pk = n. Then for each boundary p_t, target[0:p_t] is formable by first t segments, so dp[p_t] <= t. Thus all boundaries reachable. Our DP computes min over all partitions, so will find. Conversely, if dp[n] finite, there is a sequence of transitions. Good.

If target[i:j] is a prefix of word W, then target[i:k] is a prefix of target[i:j], and since target[i:j] is a prefix of W, target[i:k] is also a prefix of W. Yes. Good.

That's okay; we just can't continue from i. But if dp[i] finite and no segment starts at i, then any partition that uses i as a boundary cannot continue. However, dp[i] finite might be from a shorter prefix of a segment that actually spans over i; using i as boundary is optional. Our DP at i does nothing, but dp[n] might already be set by the longer segment from earlier. Example words ["ab"], target "ab": i=1 dp1=1, target[1]='b' not in root, no update, but dp2=1 from i=0. Good.
What if dp[n] can only be reached by a partition that uses i as a boundary, but no segment starts at i? Contradiction: if partition uses i as boundary, the next segment starts at i, so target[i] must be in trie. If no segment starts at i, no partition using i as boundary. But maybe another partition not using i as boundary reaches n. DP handles both. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "words" list maybe contains words with length > target; traversal stops at target end. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "target" length 5000; if we use `while j < n:` and `ch = target[j]`, but if node is empty, `ch not in node` false. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "minimum number" if target can be formed by concatenating valid strings but the DP returns -1 because we don't update dp[j] for j where target[i:j] is valid but j-i > max word length? Trie traversal stops at leaf, so no.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "words" sum length 1e5 and target length 5000; if we use nested dict, the trie insertion is O(total length). Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "prefix of any string in words" where the word itself might be a prefix of another word; trie handles.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "minimum number" if target can be formed by concatenating valid strings but the DP returns a value > actual because we don't allow a segment to start at i if dp[i] is INF, but dp[i] could be reachable by a segment that starts at i? no.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "words" list maybe contains words with length 0? no.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "target" length 5000; if we use `for i in range(n):` and inside while, but if dp[i] is finite and target[i] not in trie, no update. This is fine.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "minimum number" if target can be formed by concatenating valid strings but the DP returns -1 because we skip an INF position that is actually reachable via a segment from an earlier position that we didn't process because dp[earlier] was INF? impossible.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "prefix of any string in words" where the prefix can be the whole word; yes.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "minimum number" if target can be formed by concatenating valid strings but the DP returns a value > actual because we don't consider that a valid string can be a prefix of a word that is not in the trie? All words in trie. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "words" list maybe contains words with same prefix; trie merges. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "minimum number" if target can be formed by concatenating valid strings but the DP returns a value > actual because we don't allow a segment to start at i if dp[i] is INF, but dp[i] could be reachable by a segment that starts at i? no.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "words" list maybe contains words with length > target; traversal stops at target end. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "words" list maybe contains words with length 0? no.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "target" length 5000; if we use `for i in range(n):` and inside while, but if dp[i] is finite and target[i] not in trie, no update. This is fine.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "minimum number" if target can be formed by concatenating valid strings but the DP returns -1 because we skip an INF position that is actually reachable via a segment from an earlier position that we didn't process because dp[earlier] was INF? impossible.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "words" sum length 1e5 and target length 5000; if we use nested dict, the trie insertion is O(total length). Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "prefix of any string in words" where the prefix can be the whole word; yes.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "minimum number" if target can be formed by concatenating valid strings but the DP returns a value > actual because we don't consider that a valid string can be a prefix of a word that is not in the trie? All words
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minValidStrings(self, words: List[str], target: str) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for w in words:
            node = trie
            for ch in w:
                if ch not in node:
                    node[ch] = {}
                node = node[ch]

        n = len(target)
        INF = 10**9
        dp = [INF] * (n + 1)
        dp[0] = 0

        for i in range(n):
            if dp[i] == INF:
                continue

            node = trie
            j = i
            while j < n:
                ch = target[j]
                if ch not in node:
                    break
                node = node[ch]
                j += 1
                if dp[i] + 1 < dp[j]:
                    dp[j] = dp[i] + 1

        return -1 if dp[n] == INF else dp[n]
```
